## Wheatpasting

Instructions Wheatpasting, like graffiti, is a direct action technique for communicating with your
                   neighbors and redecorating your environment. Some demographics take posters more
                   seriously than spray paint, and since it is possible to mass-produce them, wheatpasting enables you to deploy a nuanced, complex message at a number of locations with
                   minimal effort and risk. Such repetition makes your message familiar to everyone and
## increases the chances that others will think it over.

    Making Paste To make wheatpaste, mix two parts white or whole-grain wheat flour with three parts
                   water, stir out any lumps, and heat the mixture to a boil. When it thickens, add more
                   water; continue cooking it on low heat for at least half an hour, stirring constantly so as
                   not to bum it. Some people add a little sugar or cornstarch for extra stickiness; don't be
                   afraid to experiment. Wheatpaste, once made, will last for a while if kept in sealed containers, though eventually it will dry up or become rotten —
## and sealed containers of it
                   have been known to burst, to unfortunate effect. Keep them in a refrigerator if you can.
                     You can also obtain wallpaper adhesive at any home improvement store; this comes
                   in pre-mixed buckets or boxes of powder. Wallpaper adhesive is much quicker and easier to mix than wheatpaste, and not much more expensive even if you are paying for it.
                   Do not get the brands that are advertised as "easy to remove," for obvious reasons get
## the most heavy-duty adhesive available.
## —
             598
If you're wheatpasting to express information or ideas, good design is key to getting Posters your message across. Remember, most people will see these from a distance, so make the headline huge and legible and use images that are simple, high-contrast, and equally large. Be sure the headline comraiunicates the basic idea on its own. You can also include
a paragraph or so in smaller print for the casually interested, and it's always a good idea to add a webpage address or similar link for those who want to pursue things further. Don't limit yourself to pasting up standard-size photocopies; many photocopying franchises offer much bigger options. You can make huge posters to put up; if such printing technology is unavailable, you can paste up big images comprised of smaller copies. You can also paste up pages of newsprint out of radical publications, or for that matter those police target-practice sheets with photos of masked men on them, or bus schedules screenprinted with artistic designs, or income tax forms stenciled with the appropriate messages about taxation, representation, and exploitation. This may seem counterintuitive, but the thinner the paper, the better —thin paper takes paste better,
and will be more likely to rip off in tiny pieces rather than all at once if an art hater takes a dislike to it. Another way to foil such philistines is to run a razor quickly down and across each poster several times immediately after you've pasted it up; a pasted poster sliced in this manner will only come down one small piece at a time.
If you're pasting up a lot of small posters, they should be easy to access without its being Technique obvious that you have them. A messenger bag works well enough for this — just make sure you can reach into it and slide one out vidthout much fumbling. If you have great big posters, roll them up, top side out so you can swiftly unroll them down the wall, and rubber band them individually. You'll need a container from which to apply the paste. Wheatpaste tends to be thick,
## wheatpasting
so a vessel with a wdde mouth such as a large plastic bottled water container is well 599
                       suited for it; wallpaper adhesive tends to be thinner and more consistent, so it can
## be
                       dispensed out of smaller holes, such as that of a dishwashing soap container with a
                       pop-up nozzle. It can help to have something to smooth the posters up on the wall —
                       window-washing squeegee from a gas station will suffice, or you could get a plastic
27. T
                       wallpaper smoother from the same retailers that provide wallpaper adhesive. Big paintbrushes can speed the application of wheatpaste, too {figure 21.1). You can do all of this
## with your hands, as well.
                          For each poster, pick a good location, and make sure it's clean; most smooth metal,
                       glass, or stucco will take pasting nicely, while wood or concrete will be somewhat
## less accommodating, and brick even less so. Next, apply paste. If you are working with smaller
                       posters, you can spread paste over the wall, place the poster on the pasted area
## and
                       smooth out all air bubbles and wrinkles, and finally spread some paste over the top to
                       hold down the comers—the more wheatpaste, the longer it will take to dry, so use the
                       minimum amount to make sure all of the poster will stick. If you are working with
                       larger posters, you can unroll them flat on the ground and apply the paste to their
## backs
                       there, before putting them on the wall, smoothing them out, and adding another
## layer
                       of paste; starting out on the ground enables you to be less conspicuous while you make
## sure the paste is evenly applied.
                          When you think about where to paste, balance the length of time the poster will probably stay up against the amount of traffic the location gets, factoring in the question
                       of which demographics will most appreciate your design; often it is better to put up a
                       poster that will remain in an alley for six months than it is to put twenty up along Main
## Street that will be gone by noon.
                         Because wheatpasting is somewhat less than legal, it is best to go about it in an inconWheatpasting
                       spicuous manner. Late in the evening, when the streets are quiet but not yet empty and
                600 you can pass yourselves off as students going to a party or workers walking back from
a bar, can be a good time for it. Behave as though what you're doing is perfectly legal, while being careful not to do it before the gaze of the authorities; you'll be surprised what you can get away with. The last time I was in a city locked under the control of thousands of riot police, many ofus were still able to thoroughly decorate whole districts with posters. A bicycle can be a useful accessory for poster pasting. You can carry your supplies in its basket, and it can function as a ladder to reach places where your art is more visible and harder to remove^ — ^not to mention assist in a quick getaway, should the need arise.
Also, bring something to clean up with—even if you wear latex gloves to keep your hands from getting sticky with wheatpaste, it can get all over your clothes, which is a dead giveaway that you're the culprit.
A well-coordinated group can cover a city in posters in the course of an evening—di- Applications vide up the area, have target locations in mind in advance, and knock out the action quickly so you've all disappeared by the time police have noticed a new poster appearing everywhere. Wheatpasting can also be applied to rework the images and messages of billboards (see Billboard Improvement, pg. 114). A group attending a massive demonstration or similar gathering could make wheatpasting kits including prepared wheatpaste, posters, and even maps showing vulnerable zones of the city, and distribute these to other groups with time and energy to apply. Best of all, you can paste up posters with wheatpaste recipes on them and a call for submissions, encouraging others to participate in decorating your town.

## Wheatpasting

Account Most wheatpasting goes so smoothly that there's not much to tell, but it's always possible to push the limits, and this is the story of a time we did just that.
                  It was the night before the one-year anniversary of September ii, 2001, and we had
               scammed over two dozen posters five feet tall and three feet wide from the local photocopying franchise with which to address the pressing issues of terrorism and war. We had
               cased our city and identified the prime locations for these, in the downtovm shopping
               district and along a few major thoroughfares. We mapped out the area and estabHshed the
               best order for visiting these locations, so we could get the most done in each section of the
               city before the police would see that we were there, and then move to another zone.
                 We divided five roles between us. One of us was to ride a bicycle, doing reconnaissance in a radius of a few blocks around every site. The other four of us would travel
               in a vehicle. This vehicle would drop off a scout to stand lookout at one end of a street,
               as most of our targets were on one-way streets, then drop off the two people who were
               to do the pasting around a corner out of sight from the target, before driving down the
               cross street to keep watch from another direction. After the two had had enough time to
               decorate the sites chosen in that area, they would meet the driver around another quiet
               comer, and the three would pick up the pedestrian lookout and move on to the next area,
               followed by the bicyclist. All of us were connected by two-way radios with earpieces,
               except one of the two pasters, so news of the movements of pohce or others could be
               immediately relayed among us. The corporate news media had made a big deal about
               the extensive security precautions that had been made for this armiversary; accordingly,
## we were taking precautions of our own.
                 We spent a couple of hours brewing wheatpaste, then went out around midnight. We
               hit all our targets dovmtown without any trouble to speak of; at one point, the bicyclist
WheatpQsting
               informed us that a police officer had stopped a motorist a few blocks away, but we did our
        6Q2 work quickly and were out of there before the police car moved. I was one of the pasters;
having done some smaller-scale wheatpasting that had involved a lot of anxiety about whether or not I could pass as a law-abiding citizen, it was actually something of a relief to be running around in all black clothes with huge plastic jugs of wheatpaste and rolled posters—there wasn't any posing or passing to worry about, everything was on the table and it was just a matter of moving fast and staying aware. We dashed past a civihan at one point, and I said hello —
## he just stared at us like we were Martian invaders.
The last target was a freeway underpass, where eight columns held up the other highway; we were to put up eight posters, four facing each traffic direction. We were starting to encounter some problems at this point, after so much success: our clothes had inevitably been covered in wheatpaste, and in fact it was starting to clog the microphone and earpiece of the two-way radio we carried with us. All the same, the scouts took their positions and we were dropped off next to the underpass to finish the job. Ducking down whenever cars came and then leaping up to apply the posters, we did four columns, and then leaped over the concrete guardwall to run across the freeway; in a scene out of a slapstick comedy, I was holding the wheatpaste in one hand and the posters in the other, and so had to
hurtle myself over the wall, crashing absurdly on the asphalt without my hands to break my fall. My friend helped me over the other wall, and we began the fifth column. At this point our radio made some kind of noise, but it was impossible to make out the words through the wheatpaste. An instant later, headlights appeared, and we got down behind the column, moving slowly around it as the car approached and passed us. It was a police car. It kept going, so we set back to work wheatpasting, but no sooner had
we done so than headlights appeared from the opposite direction, and we had to work our way around the pillar again, hiding as another police car drove by. This was starting to look bad. It was impossible now to get our radio to work, so, abandoning the posters, we set out walking quickly away from the underpass. As more headlights appeared
## Wheatpasting
ahead, I tossed the last jug of wheatpaste into the bushes. 603
                 We turned down a side street as soon as we could; in our black camouflage with paste
               stains all over —note that wheatpaste stains more visibly on dark colors — we looked
               more than a little suspicious, especially this late at night in a district with no pedestrian
               traffic. Worse yet, it turned out the street we had turned down was a long corridor with

## —
               no exits on the sides, running through a closed warehouse district no alibi could adequately explain our presence here. At that moment, a police car turned onto the street,
               and slowed to a crawl as it approached us. We kept walking, maintaining our conversation as calmly as we could, acting as though we were oblivious to the pohceman as he
## inched past, blatantly staring at us.
                 Strangely, he kept going! Seeing that we had no posters or paste, he must not have felt
## that he had enough evidence to justify arresting us —
## though the stains on our clothes
               would have given us away on closer inspection. We made our way down other side
               streets and walked all the way back to our secret hideout, where the others were waiting,
               relieved that we had escaped and excited to teU us about the police cars that had started
## following them and forced them to abandon us.
                 We slept a scant few hours, then went out shortly after morning rush hour to inspect
               our work. It turned out we had undone ourselves with our overachieving; had we only
               put up standard-sized posters, they would have stayed up in some areas for a long time,
               but the city's entire anti-terror force, having nothing else to do but intimidate citizens
               and squelch free expression, had spent the whole night working to remove our massive
## art installations. That was demoralizing!

                 Fortunately, we weren't the only ones on the streets that night. We soon learned that
               our friends in the larger city nearby had spent the evening the same way, and their posters stayed up for weeks afterwards —including a few facing the city's busiest freeway!

## IMPERIAL SYSTEM ^^H

Book constructed by Paul F. Maul Artists' Group workers Turkish, F. Mark, and Tim, with guest appearances by the Steamroller, Skye, andthe Dirty Harry. Essential sonic fuels provided throughout the long ordeal by the Decemberists, Mirah, They Might Be Giants, and Jon Brion—thanks.
The type of this book is set exclusively in the Scala family of fonts created in Utrecht, Holland by Martin Majoor in 1990. It has a great *fj' ligature that I didn't get to use even once in this whole book. Members of this wonderfully expansive family appearing within include: Scala, Scala Italic, Scala Bold, Scala Caps, Scala Sans Caps,
Scala Sans Light, Scaia Sans, Scala Sam Italic, Scala Sans Bold Italic, Scala Sans Black, and Scala Sans Black Italic.
The typeface used on the cover and back cover is Futura Heavy, designed by Paul Renner in Germany in 1928, inspired by, but decidedly not part of, the Bauhaus movement. Its mechanical and geometric beauty is balanced by its quirky and asymmetrical nuances, making it the Mona Lisa of modem typeface design.

## I

When the world ends, people come out of their apartments and meet their neighbors for the first time; they share food, stories, companionship.
## No one has to go to work or the
laundromat; nobody remembers to check the mirror or scale or email
## account before leaving
the house. Graffiti artists surge into the streets; strangers
## embrace, sobbing and laughing.
Every moment possesses an immediacy formerly spread out
## across months. Burdens fall
away, people confess secrets and grant forgiveness, the stars
## come out over New York City . . .

. .*.

## *
              •

           4 nine months later a new generation is born.
r You must always have a secret plan. Everything depends on this: it is the only question. So as not to be conquered by the conquered territory in which you lead your life, so as not to feel the horrible weight of inertia wrecking your will and bending you to the ground, so as not to spend a single night more wondering what there is to do or how to connect with your neighbors and countrymen, you
    must make secret plans without respite. Plan for adventure, plan for pleasure,
    plan for pandemonium, as you wish; but plan, lay plans constantly.
         And when you come to, on the steps of the presidential palace, in the green
    gross beside the highway, in your cell's gloomy solitude, your secret plan finished
    or foiled, ask your comrades, ask your cellmates, ask the wind, the waves, the
    stars, the sea, ask everything that ponders, everything that wanders, everything
    that sings, everything that stings —ask them what time it is; and your comrades,
    your cellmates, the wind, the waves, the stars, the sea all will answer: ''It is
    time for a new secret plan. So as not to be the martyred slave of routine, plan
    adventure, plan pleasure, plan pandemonium, as you wish; but plan, plan
    secretly and without respite." y% I
